  "question": "where is the horizontal scroll feature implemented?",
  "answer": "The horizontal scroll feature is implemented in the `HorizontalScrollFeatures` component, which is located in the `components/main/features` directory. This component is imported and used in the `Home` function in the `app/page.tsx` file.\n\nThe `HorizontalScrollFeatures` component contains two main sections: `intro` and `parallax-zoom`. The `intro` section contains an `AnimatedText` component, and the `parallax-zoom` section contains a `ParallaxZoom` component.\n\nThe horizontal scrolling effect is achieved through the use of CSS and JavaScript. The `HorizontalScroll` component, which is used in the `ParallaxZoom` component, utilizes the `motion` library from Framer Motion to animate the scrolling effect.\n\nSpecifically, the `HorizontalScroll` component uses the `useTransform` hook from Framer Motion to transform the x-coordinate of the scrolling elements based on the scroll progress. The `useScroll` hook is used to get the scroll progress, and the `useTransform` hook is used to transform the x-coordinate of the elements.\n\nHere is an example of the relevant code snippet from the `HorizontalScroll` component:\n```tsx\nimport { motion, useTransform } from \"framer-motion\";\nimport { useScroll } from \"framer-motion\";\n\nconst HorizontalScroll = () => {\n  const { scrollYProgress } = useScroll({\n    target: containerRef,\n    offset: [\"start start\", \"end end\"],\n  });\n\n  const x = useTransform(scrollYProgress, [0.5, 1], [\"0px\", `-${offsetSize}px`]);\n\n  return (\n    <div className=\"w-full h-full p-3 overflow-hidden\">\n      <motion.div ref={translateRef} className=\"w-full flex h-full\" style={{ x }}>\n        {/* scrolling elements */}\n      </motion.div>\n    </div>\n  );\n};\n```\nIn this code snippet, the `useScroll` hook is used to get the scroll progress, and the `useTransform` hook is used to transform the x-coordinate of the scrolling elements based on the scroll progress. The `style` attribute is used to apply the transformed x-coordinate to the scrolling elements.\n\nOverall, the horizontal scroll feature is implemented through a combination of CSS, JavaScript, and the use of libraries like Framer Motion.",

**Question:** "Where is the horizontal scroll feature implemented?"

**AI-Generated Answer:**

The horizontal scroll feature is implemented in the [`HorizontalScrollFeatures`](jitter-landing-clone/components/main/features/HorizontalScrollFeatures.tsx) component, located in `components/main/features/` directory.

**Key Implementation Details:**

1. **Main Component:** [`HorizontalScrollFeatures.tsx`](jitter-landing-clone/components/main/features/HorizontalScrollFeatures.tsx)
   - Contains two sections: `intro` and `parallax-zoom`
   - Used in [`app/page.tsx`](jitter-landing-clone/app/page.tsx)

2. **Core Scrolling Logic:** [`HorizontalScroll.tsx`](jitter-landing-clone/components/main/features/HorizontalScroll.tsx)
   - Uses Framer Motion's `motion` library
   - Implements `useScroll` hook to track scroll progress
   - Uses `useTransform` hook to animate x-coordinate based on scroll

3. **Code Example:**
```tsx
const { scrollYProgress } = useScroll({
  target: containerRef,
  offset: ["start start", "end end"],
});

const x = useTransform(scrollYProgress, [0.5, 1], ["0px", `-${offsetSize}px`]);
```

**Related Files:**
- [`AnimatedText.tsx`](jitter-landing-clone/components/main/features/AnimatedText.tsx)
- [`ParallaxZoom.tsx`](jitter-landing-clone/components/main/features/ParallaxZoom.tsx)

  1 | """
  2 | MCP tool for generating onboarding guides from repository analysis.
  3 | """
  4 | 
  5 | import asyncio
  6 | from .fetch_repo import fetch_repo_tool
  7 | from ..storage.context_store import ContextStore
  8 | from ..utils.validators import validate_repo_url, parse_github_url
  9 | 
 10 | # Try to import watsonx summarizer, but don't fail if not configured

 13 |     WATSONX_AVAILABLE = True

 18 | def _build_guide_prompt(repo_name: str, context_string: str, tech_stack: dict) -> str:
 19 |     """
 20 |     Build the prompt for WatsonX to generate the onboarding guide.
 21 |     
 22 |     Args:
 23 |         repo_name: Name of the repository
 24 |         context_string: Full context string with code snippets
 25 |         tech_stack: Tech stack analysis dictionary
 26 |     
 27 |     Returns:
 28 |         Formatted prompt string for LLM
 29 |     """
 30 |     frameworks = tech_stack.get("frameworks", {})
 31 |     framework_str = ", ".join([
 32 |         item for sublist in frameworks.values() for item in sublist
 33 |     ]) or "None detected"
 34 | 
 35 |     return f"""You are an expert software architect creating a developer onboarding guide.
 36 | Analyze the repository code provided and generate a comprehensive onboarding guide.
 37 | Be SPECIFIC — reference actual file names, function names, and line numbers from the code.
 38 | Never give generic advice. If you cannot find something in the code, say so honestly.
 39 | 
 40 | REPOSITORY: {repo_name}
 41 | PRIMARY LANGUAGE: {tech_stack.get('primary_language', 'Unknown')}
 42 | FRAMEWORKS: {framework_str}
 43 | HAS TESTS: {tech_stack.get('has_tests', False)}
 44 | HAS DOCKER: {tech_stack.get('has_docker', False)}
 45 | HAS CI: {tech_stack.get('has_ci', False)}
 46 | ARCHITECTURE: {tech_stack.get('architecture', 'standard')}
 47 | 
 48 | CODEBASE:
 49 | {context_string}
 50 | 
 51 | Generate a developer onboarding guide with EXACTLY these sections in this order.
 52 | Use the actual code above — do not make anything up:
 53 | 
 54 | ## Project Overview
 55 | What this project does in 2-3 sentences. What problem it solves. Who uses it.
 56 | 
 57 | ## Tech Stack
 58 | List every technology detected with a one-line explanation of its role in THIS project.
 59 | 
 60 | ## Architecture
 61 | Explain how the system is structured in 3-4 sentences referencing actual folders and files.
 62 | Then provide a Mermaid diagram showing the main components and how they connect.
 63 | Format the diagram as:
 64 | ```mermaid
 65 | graph TD
 66 |     ...
 67 | ```
 68 | 
 69 | ## Key Modules
 70 | For each major folder or module: name, file path, what it does, and its 2-3 most important files.
 71 | 
 72 | ## Getting Started
 73 | Exact commands to clone, install dependencies and run the project locally.
 74 | List prerequisites. Include any gotchas or non-obvious steps you can see from the code.
 75 | 
 76 | ## Entry Points
 77 | Where does the application start? List the main entry files with their exact paths and what happens when they run.
 78 | 
 79 | ## First Week Tasks
 80 | List 3 specific tasks a new developer should do in their first week.
 81 | Each task must reference actual files they will touch and explain why it is a good starting task.
 82 | 
 83 | ## Glossary
 84 | List project-specific terms, abbreviations, or patterns used in this codebase that a new developer needs to know.
 85 | 
 86 | Be specific. Be direct. Reference real file paths. Do not pad with generic advice."""
 87 | 
 88 | 
 89 | async def _call_watsonx(prompt: str) -> str:
 90 |     """
 91 |     Call WatsonX to generate guide content.
 92 |     
 93 |     Args:
 94 |         prompt: The formatted prompt for guide generation
 95 |     
 96 |     Returns:
 97 |         Generated guide markdown string, or error string starting with "ERROR:"
 98 |     """
 99 |     try:
100 |         from ibm_watsonx_ai.foundation_models import ModelInference
101 |         from ..config import (
102 |             WATSONX_API_KEY,
103 |             WATSONX_PROJECT_ID,
104 |             WATSONX_URL,
105 |             WATSONX_MODEL_ID,
106 |             WATSONX_MAX_TOKENS
107 |         )
108 |     except ImportError as e:
109 |         return f"ERROR: WatsonX library not available: {str(e)}"
110 |     
111 |     try:
112 |         # Create model instance
113 |         model = ModelInference(
114 |             model_id=WATSONX_MODEL_ID,
115 |             credentials={"apikey": WATSONX_API_KEY, "url": WATSONX_URL},
116 |             project_id=WATSONX_PROJECT_ID
117 |         )
118 |         
119 |         # Run synchronous generate_text in executor to avoid blocking
120 |         loop = asyncio.get_event_loop()
121 |         generated_text = await loop.run_in_executor(
122 |             None,
123 |             lambda: model.chat(
124 |                 messages=[{"role": "user", "content": prompt}],
125 |                 params={"max_new_tokens": WATSONX_MAX_TOKENS}
126 |             )
127 |         )
128 | 
129 |         return generated_text["choices"][0]["message"]["content"]
130 |     
131 |     except Exception as e:
132 |         return f"ERROR: Failed to generate guide with WatsonX: {str(e)}"
133 | 
134 | 
135 | def _build_fallback_guide(repo_name: str, repo_url: str, tech_stack: dict) -> str:
136 |     """
137 |     Build a fallback guide using structured template when WatsonX is unavailable.
138 |     
139 |     Args:
140 |         repo_name: Name of the repository
141 |         repo_url: Full repository URL
142 |         tech_stack: Tech stack analysis dictionary
143 |     
144 |     Returns:
145 |         Structured markdown guide template
146 |     """
147 |     return f"""# {repo_name} - Developer Onboarding Guide
148 | 
149 | ## Project Overview
150 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
151 | 
152 | ## Tech Stack
153 | **Primary Language:** {tech_stack.get('primary_language', 'Unknown')}
154 | 
155 | **All Languages:** {', '.join(tech_stack.get('all_languages', []))}
156 | 
157 | **Frameworks:**
158 | {_format_frameworks(tech_stack.get('frameworks', {}))}
159 | 
160 | **Dependencies:**
161 | {_format_dependencies(tech_stack.get('dependencies', {}))}
162 | 
163 | ## Architecture
164 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
165 | 
166 | {_generate_architecture_diagram()}
167 | 
168 | ## Key Modules
169 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
170 | 
171 | ## Getting Started
172 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
173 | 
174 | ## Entry Points
175 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
176 | 
177 | ## First Week Tasks
178 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
179 | 
180 | ## Glossary
181 | *AI generation unavailable — configure WATSONX_API_KEY for full AI-generated content.*
182 | 
183 | ---
184 | *Generated by CodeOnboard MCP Server*
185 | *Repository: {repo_url}*
186 | *Total Files Analyzed: {tech_stack.get('total_files', 0)}*
187 | *Has Tests: {'Yes' if tech_stack.get('has_tests') else 'No'}*
188 | *Has Docker: {'Yes' if tech_stack.get('has_docker') else 'No'}*
189 | *Has CI/CD: {'Yes' if tech_stack.get('has_ci') else 'No'}*
190 | *Architecture: {tech_stack.get('architecture', 'standard').title()}*
191 | """
192 | 
193 | 
194 | async def generate_guide_tool(repo_url: str, include_diagrams: bool = True) -> dict:
195 |     """
196 |     Generate a comprehensive onboarding guide for a repository.
197 |     
198 |     This tool:
199 |     1. Validates the repository URL
200 |     2. Checks if repo is cached (fetches if not)
201 |     3. Gets context string and tech stack from cache
202 |     4. Generates a structured markdown guide with predefined sections
203 |     5. Optionally includes Mermaid diagrams
204 |     
205 |     Args:
206 |         repo_url: GitHub repository URL
207 |         include_diagrams: Whether to include architecture diagrams (default: True)
208 |     
209 |     Returns:
210 |         Dictionary with the generated guide and metadata
211 |     """
212 |     try:
213 |         # Validate URL
214 |         is_valid, error_msg = validate_repo_url(repo_url)
215 |         if not is_valid:
216 |             return {
217 |                 "success": False,
218 |                 "error": error_msg
219 |             }
220 |         
221 |         # Extract repo_key
222 |         owner, repo_name = parse_github_url(repo_url)
223 |         repo_key = f"{owner}/{repo_name}"
224 |         
225 |         # Check if repo is in cache
226 |         store = ContextStore()
227 |         cached_data = store.get(repo_key)
228 |         
229 |         if not cached_data:
230 |             # Fetch repo first
231 |             fetch_result = await fetch_repo_tool(repo_url)
232 |             if not fetch_result.get("success"):
233 |                 return {
234 |                     "success": False,
235 |                     "error": f"Failed to fetch repository: {fetch_result.get('error', 'Unknown error')}"
236 |                 }
237 |             # Get cached data after fetch
238 |             cached_data = store.get(repo_key)
239 |         
240 |         # Get context string and tech stack
241 |         context_string = store.get_context_string(repo_key)
242 |         tech_stack = cached_data["tech_stack"]
243 |         
244 |         if not context_string:
245 |             return {
246 |                 "success": False,
247 |                 "error": "No repository context available"
248 |             }
249 |         
250 |         # Check if context is very large (over 40,000 chars)
251 |         effective_context = context_string
252 |         used_watsonx = False
253 |         
254 |         if len(context_string) > 40000 and WATSONX_AVAILABLE:
255 |             try:
256 |                 effective_context = await summarize_context(context_string)
257 |                 used_watsonx = True
258 |             except Exception:
259 |                 # Silently fall back to original context if summarization fails
260 |                 pass
261 |         
262 |         # Build prompt and generate guide with WatsonX
263 |         prompt = _build_guide_prompt(repo_name, effective_context, tech_stack)
264 |         guide_markdown = await _call_watsonx(prompt)
265 |         
266 |         # Check if WatsonX call failed, use fallback template
267 |         if guide_markdown.startswith("ERROR:"):
268 |             guide_markdown = _build_fallback_guide(repo_name, repo_url, tech_stack)
269 |         
270 |         sections = [
271 |             "Project Overview",
272 |             "Tech Stack",
273 |             "Architecture",
274 |             "Key Modules",
275 |             "Getting Started",
276 |             "Entry Points",
277 |             "First Week Tasks",
278 |             "Glossary"
279 |         ]
280 |         
281 |         return {
282 |             "success": True,
283 |             "repo_key": repo_key,
284 |             "guide_markdown": guide_markdown,
285 |             "sections": sections,
286 |             "tech_stack": tech_stack,
287 |             "used_watsonx": used_watsonx,
288 |             "message": f"Guide generated successfully{' (using WatsonX summarizer for large context)' if used_watsonx else ''}"
289 |         }
290 |     
291 |     except Exception as e:
292 |         return {
293 |             "success": False,
294 |             "error": f"Failed to generate guide: {str(e)}"
295 |         }
296 | 
297 | 
298 | def _format_frameworks(frameworks: dict) -> str:
299 |     """Format frameworks dict as markdown list."""
300 |     if not frameworks:
301 |         return "None detected"
302 |     
303 |     result = []
304 |     for category, items in frameworks.items():
305 |         if items:
306 |             result.append(f"- **{category.replace('_', ' ').title()}:** {', '.join(items)}")
307 |     
308 |     return "\n".join(result) if result else "None detected"
309 | 
310 | 
311 | def _format_dependencies(dependencies: dict) -> str:
312 |     """Format dependencies dict as markdown list."""
313 |     if not dependencies:
314 |         return "None detected"
315 |     
316 |     result = []
317 |     for file_name, deps in dependencies.items():
318 |         if deps:
319 |             result.append(f"- **{file_name}:** {len(deps)} dependencies")
320 |     
321 |     return "\n".join(result) if result else "None detected"
322 | 
323 | 
324 | def _generate_architecture_diagram() -> str:
325 |     """Generate a placeholder for architecture diagram."""
326 |     return """
327 | ### Architecture Diagram
328 | ```mermaid
329 | graph TD
330 |     A[TODO: Generate architecture diagram]
331 |     B[Using Mermaid syntax]
332 |     A --> B
333 | ```
334 | """
335 | 
336 | # Made with Bob

  "guide_markdown": "## Project Overview\nThe Jitter-landing-clone project is a web application built using Next.js and React, aiming to create a clone of a video landing page. It solves the problem of providing a high-performance and visually appealing landing page for video content. The users of this project are likely developers and designers who want to create a similar landing page for their own video content.\n\n## Tech Stack\n* Next.js: a React-based framework for building server-rendered, statically generated, and performance-optimized web applications.\n* React: a JavaScript library for building user interfaces.\n* TypeScript: a superset of JavaScript that adds optional static typing and other features.\n\n## Architecture\nThe system is structured around the standard Next.js directory layout, with components, pages, and utilities organized in separate folders. The `app/layout.tsx` file defines the root layout component, which wraps the entire application. The `components` folder contains various components that make up the main content of the page. The `app/globals.css` file defines global CSS styles.\n```mermaid\ngraph TD\n    A[app/layout.tsx] -->|wraps|> B[app/page.tsx]\n    B -->|renders|> C[components/header/Header.tsx]\n    B -->|renders|> D[components/main]\n    D -->|contains|> E[components/main/AnimatedText]\n    D -->|contains|> F[components/main/HorizontalScroll]\n    D -->|contains|> G[components/main/ParallaxZoom]\n    D -->|contains|> H[components/main/StickyCards]\n```\n\n## Key Modules\n* **Layout**: `app/layout.tsx`, defines the root layout component, key files: `app/layout.tsx`, `app/globals.css`.\n* **Page**: `app/page.tsx`, represents the main page component, key files: `app/page.tsx`, `components/header/Header.tsx`.\n* **Components**: `components`, contains various components, key files: `components/header/Header.tsx`, `components/main/AnimatedText`.\n* **Utilities**: `hooks`, provides custom hooks, key files: `hooks/viewportSize.tsx`.\n\n## Getting Started\nTo get started, clone the repository using `git clone https://github.com/username/Jitter-landing-clone.git`. Then, install dependencies using `npm install` or `yarn install`. Run the project locally using `npm run dev` or `yarn dev`. Prerequisites: Node.js, npm or yarn. Gotcha: make sure to install the correct version of Node.js and npm or yarn.\n\n## Entry Points\nThe main entry files are `app/layout.tsx` and `app/page.tsx`. When `app/layout.tsx` runs, it wraps the entire application and renders the `app/page.tsx` component. When `app/page.tsx` runs, it renders the header, main content, and footer.\n\n## First Week Tasks\n1. Update the `app/globals.css` file to change the global typography styles, which will help you understand how CSS is structured in the project. This task is a good starting point because it allows you to familiarize yourself with the project's CSS architecture.\n2. Modify the `components/header/Header.tsx` file to add a new navigation menu item, which will help you understand how components interact with each other. This task is a good starting point because it allows you to see how props are passed between components.\n3. Implement a new animation in the `components/main/AnimatedText` file using Framer Motion, which will help you understand how animations are created in the project. This task is a good starting point because it allows you to learn about the animation libraries used in the project.\n\n## Glossary\n* **Layout**: refers to the root layout component defined in `app/layout.tsx`.\n* **Page**: refers to the main page component defined in `app/page.tsx`.\n* **Components**: refers to the various components contained in the `components` folder.\n* **Viewport size**: refers to the custom hook provided in `hooks/viewportSize.tsx` to get the current viewport size.",

The target users of this project are likely customers or potential customers of Jitter Video who want to learn more about their services.\n\n## Tech Stack\n1. Next.js: A React-based framework for building server-side rendered (SSR) and statically generated websites and applications.\n2. TypeScript: A programming language used for type checking and code completion.\n3. ESLint: A linter used for code quality and formatting.\n4. Tailwind CSS: A utility-first CSS framework used for styling.\n5. Framer Motion: An animation library used for animations and transitions.\n\n## Architecture\nThe system is structured into separate folders for components, including `app`, `components`, and `hooks`. The `app` folder contains the main application components, such as `layout.tsx` and `page.tsx`. The `components` folder is further divided into subfolders for specific components, like `header` and `main`. The `hooks` folder contains reusable hooks, such as `viewportSize.tsx`.\n```mermaid\ngraph TD\n    A[app] -->|contains|> B[layout.tsx]\n    A -->|contains|> C[page.tsx]\n    D[components] -->|contains|> E[header]\n    D -->|contains|> F[main]\n    G[hooks] -->|contains|> H[viewportSize.tsx]\n    E -->|interacts with|> B\n    F -->|interacts with|> C\n    H -->|used in|> E\n    H -->|used in|> F\n```\n\n## Key Modules\n1. **App Module**: `app/`, contains the main application components, including `layout.tsx` and `page.tsx`.\n2. **Components Module**: `components/`, contains reusable components, including `header` and `main`. Important files: `components/header/Header.tsx`, `components/main/features/HorizontalScrollFeatures.tsx`.\n3. **Hooks Module**: `hooks/`, contains reusable hooks, including `viewportSize.tsx`. Important file: `hooks/viewportSize.tsx`.\n\n## Getting Started\nTo get started, clone the repository using `git clone https://github.com/username/Jitter-landing-clone.git`. Then, install dependencies using `npm install` or `yarn install`. Finally, run the project locally using `npm run dev` or `yarn dev`. Prerequisites: Node.js, npm or yarn. Gotcha: Make sure to install the correct version of Node.js and npm or yarn, as specified in the project's `package.json` file.\n\n## Entry Points\nThe main entry files are `app/layout.tsx` and `app/page.tsx`. When `app/layout.tsx` runs, it renders the root layout component, wrapping the entire application. When `app/page.tsx` runs, it renders the main page component, including the header and main content.\n\n## First Week Tasks\n1. **Update the Header Component**: Open `components/header/Header.tsx` and update the navigation menu to include a new item. This task is a good starting point because it allows you to familiarize yourself with the component structure and styling.\n2. **Implement a New Feature**: Open `components/main/features/HorizontalScrollFeatures.tsx` and implement a new feature, such as a vertical scroll functionality. <path>README.md</path>
<content># CodeOnboard

AI-powered developer onboarding that turns any GitHub repository into a comprehensive guide in seconds.

## What It Does

New developers joining a project waste hours reading scattered documentation and exploring unfamiliar codebases. CodeOnboard solves this by automatically analyzing any GitHub repository and generating a structured onboarding guide with architecture diagrams, setup instructions, and first-week tasks. It uses IBM watsonx AI to understand the codebase and answer follow-up questions with specific file references, turning days of exploration into minutes of focused learning.

## How It Works

Short architecture explanation with this flow diagram:

```
Developer types in Bob IDE
        ↓
Bob calls CodeOnboard MCP tools
        ↓
fetch_repo → GitHub API → analyzes tech stack
        ↓
generate_guide → watsonx AI → writes ONBOARDING.md
        ↓
ask_codebase → answers follow-up questions with file references
```

## MCP Tools

| Tool | What it does | When to use it |
|------|-------------|----------------|
| `fetch_repo` | Fetches repository from GitHub, analyzes file structure, detects tech stack, frameworks, and dependencies | First step before generating guides or asking questions about a new repository |
| `generate_guide` | Uses watsonx AI to generate comprehensive onboarding guide with architecture, setup steps, key modules, and first-week tasks | When you need a complete developer onboarding document for a repository |
| `ask_codebase` | Answers specific questions about the codebase using AI with file references and code snippets | When you need to understand specific features, patterns, or implementations in the code |

## Setup

### Prerequisites

- Python 3.11+
- IBM Bob IDE
- GitHub Personal Access Token
- IBM watsonx credentials

### Installation

```bash
git clone https://github.com/thedunncodes/codeonboard
cd codeonboard
pip install -r requirements.txt
cp .env.example .env
# Fill in your credentials in .env
```

### Connect to Bob IDE

Add this to your Bob IDE MCP settings:

```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "codeonboard": {
      "command": "python",
      "args": ["run_server.py"],
      "cwd": "/path/to/codeonboard",
      "env": {
        "GITHUB_TOKEN": "${env:GITHUB_TOKEN}",
        "WATSONX_API_KEY": "${env:WATSONX_API_KEY}",
        "WATSONX_PROJECT_ID": "${env:WATSONX_PROJECT_ID}",
        "WATSONX_URL": "${env:WATSONX_URL}"
      }
    }
  }
}
```

## Demo

Example Bob IDE prompts:

1. "Onboard me onto https://github.com/owner/repo"
2. "Generate an onboarding guide for this repository"
3. "Where is authentication handled in this codebase?"

## Tech Stack

- Python
- FastMCP
- IBM Bob IDE
- IBM watsonx AI (meta-llama/llama-3-3-70b-instruct)
- GitHub REST API
- SQLite

## Built With IBM Bob 🤖

This entire project was developed in partnership with IBM Bob as the AI coding assistant. Bob handled planning, project scaffolding, code generation, debugging, and code review throughout the development process. Every iteration and refinement is documented in the `bob_sessions/` folder, showcasing Bob's capabilities as a development partner.

## IBM Bob Hackathon 2026

Built for the IBM Bob Hackathon, May 15-17 2026 on lablab.ai
</content>
<line_count>95</line_count>
